Cycles of Well-Linked Sets II: an Elementary Bound for the Directed Grid Theorem Thanks: The results in this manuscript were also presented in Milani’s PhD thesis [ 26 ] . A preliminary version of this paper was published at FOCS 2024 [ 15 ] .
Meike Hatzel Technical University Darmstadt, Germany research@meikehatzel.com Stephan Kreutzer Technische Universität Berlin stephan.kreutzer@tu-berlin.de Marcelo Garlet Milani National Institute of Informatics, Tokyo, Japan research@mgarletmilani.com Irene Muzi Universität Hamburg irene.muzi@gmail.com

Abstract
In 2015, Kawarabayashi and Kreutzer proved the Directed Grid Theorem — the generalisation of the well-known Excluded Grid Theorem to directed graphs — confirming a conjecture by Reed, Johnson, Robertson, Seymour and Thomas from the mid-nineties. The theorem states that there is a function f f such that every digraph of directed treewidth f ⁡ ( k ) f(k) contains a cylindrical grid of order k k as a butterfly minor. However, the given function grows faster than any non-elementary function of the size of the grid minor. More precisely, it is larger than a power tower whose height depends on the size of the grid. In this paper, we present an alternative proof of the Directed Grid Theorem which is conceptually much simpler, more modular in composition and improves the upper bound for the function f f to a power tower of height 22. A key concept of our proof is a new structure called cycles of well-linked sets (CWS) . We show that any digraph of large directed treewidth contains a large CWS, which in turn contains a large cylindrical grid.



1
Introduction

The Excluded Grid Theorem by Robertson and Seymour is a central result in the study of graph minors and is the first major building block of their Graph Minors project
[
29
]
.
Additionally, the theorem has found numerous applications beyond its original scope, for instance in the theory of graph algorithms (see, for example,
[
8
]
).

Based on a conjecture by Reed, Johnson, Robertson, Seymour and Thomas from the mid-nineties
[
19
]
, Kawarabayashi and Kreutzer proved in 2015
[
21
]
an Excluded Grid Theorem for directed graphs, that is, the existence of a function
f
such that every digraph of directed treewidth
f(k)
contains a
cylindrical grid
of order
k
as a butterfly minor.
In addition, they provided an XP algorithm which produces a directed tree-decomposition of width at most
f(k)
or finds a cylindrical grid of order
k
as a butterfly minor.
Campos et al.
[
5
]
improved their result from XP to FPT.

The Directed Grid Theorem has been used to prove advanced results in digraph structure theory
[
11
,
13
,
12
]
, Erdős-Pósa/cycle packing
[
24
,
3
,
1
,
20
]
and matching theory
[
17
,
14
]
as well as for algorithmic results
[
4
,
9
]
.
Intuitively, the Directed Grid Theorem yields a
win-win
situation.
If a digraph contains a large cylindrical grid as a butterfly minor, the grid’s uniform structure can be useful in solving the task at hand.
Otherwise, if the digraph does not contain a large cylindrical grid as a butterfly minor, we know it has bounded directed treewidth and we can use its directed tree-decomposition.

As the original function
f
by Kawarabayashi and Kreutzer is very large (specifically, it grows faster than a tower whose height is dependent on the size of the grid), improving it has a major impact on all results which depend on it.
It is thus natural to ask what the best bound for
f
is.
Indeed, already determining whether it is an
elementary
function
1
1

1

In rough terms, a function is elementary if it can be constructed using a finite sequence of arithmetic operations, exponentials, logarithms and trigonometric functions, starting from constants and variables.

would vastly improve upon the bound given by
[
21
]
.
Further, improving the bounds of the Directed Grid Theorem may require the development of powerful frameworks whose usefulness extends beyond their initial scope, as was observed with the path of sets-system framework introduced by
[
6
,
7
]
in order to obtain polynomial bounds for the (undirected) Grid Theorem.

Our proof starts with a result from
[
16
]
, where we introduced the concept of
paths of order-linked sets, paths of well-linked sets
and
cycles of well-linked sets
and obtained an elementary bound for Younger’s conjecture, improving upon the non-elementary bound given by
[
27
]
.

On a high level, a path of well-linked sets consists of an ordered set of clusters, each in turn consisting of two sets of vertices
A
and
B
, where
A
is well-linked to
B
such that each cluster is connected to the next by a linkage and all the clusters and linkages are pairwise internally vertex-disjoint.
For the path of well-linked sets we obtain in
[
16
]
, we have the additional property that the last cluster is well-linked back to the first one.
However, the linkages connecting these two clusters may arbitrarily intersect the path of well-linked sets.

In
[
16
]
, we also introduced a framework based on temporal digraphs in order to construct a path of well-linked sets and a path of order-linked sets in digraphs of large directed treewidth.
We then showed that we can obtain
fences
and
acyclic grids
from these two objects.

This paper builds upon the abovementioned work and proves an elementary upper bound for the Directed Grid Theorem.
Our starting point is the following statement (
\operatorname{exp}_{2}^{h}(n)
denotes a power tower of height
h
with base 2 and an
n
“on top”, and
\operatorname{poly}^{d}(x_{1},\ldots,x_{k})
denotes a polynomial of degree
d
on the variables
x_{1},\ldots,x_{k}
).

Theorem 1.1

(
[
T
]
heorem 9.9)
.

COSSI]

There exists a function
{\color[rgb]{0.05,0.375,0.0375}\mathsf{t}_{\ref{thm:high_dtw_to_POSS_plus_back-linkage}}}\mathchoice{\left(w,\ell\right)}{(w,\ell)}{(w,\ell)}{(w,\ell)}\in\operatorname{exp}_{2}^{7}(\operatorname{poly}^{25}(w,\ell))
such that every digraph
D
with
\operatorname{dtw}\mathchoice{\left(D\right)}{(D)}{(D)}{(D)}\geq{\color[rgb]{0.05,0.375,0.0375}\mathsf{t}_{\ref{thm:high_dtw_to_POSS_plus_back-linkage}}}\mathchoice{\left(w,\ell\right)}{(w,\ell)}{(w,\ell)}{(w,\ell)}
contains a path of well-linked sets
(\mathcal{S}=(S_{0},S_{1},\dots,

S_{\ell}),\mathscr{P})
of width
w
and length
\ell
and, additionally,
B(S_{\ell})
is well-linked to
A(S_{0})
in
D
.

Our first technical contribution is to prove that a digraph containing a path of well-linked sets as described above also contains a cycle of well-linked sets.
A cycle of well-linked sets consists of a path of well-linked sets together with a linkage
\mathcal{L}
from the last to the first cluster, where
\mathcal{L}
is internally disjoint from the path of well-linked sets.
The functions

{\color[rgb]{0.05,0.375,0.0375}\mathsf{w^{\prime}}_{\ref{thm:POSS_plus_back-linkage_to_COSS}}}
,

{\color[rgb]{0.05,0.375,0.0375}\mathsf{r}_{\ref{thm:POSS_plus_back-linkage_to_COSS}}}
and

{\color[rgb]{0.05,0.375,0.0375}\mathsf{\ell^{\prime}}_{\ref{thm:POSS_plus_back-linkage_to_COSS}}}

\displaystyle\in\operatorname{exp}_{2}^{2}(\operatorname{poly}^{97}(\ell,w)),

\displaystyle{\color[rgb]{0.05,0.375,0.0375}\mathsf{r}_{\ref{thm:POSS_plus_back-linkage_to_COSS}}}\mathchoice{\left(w,\ell\right)}{(w,\ell)}{(w,\ell)}{(w,\ell)}

\displaystyle\in\operatorname{exp}_{2}^{13}(\operatorname{poly}^{97}(\ell,w))\text{ and }

\displaystyle{\color[rgb]{0.05,0.375,0.0375}\mathsf{\ell^{\prime}}_{\ref{thm:POSS_plus_back-linkage_to_COSS}}}\mathchoice{\left(w,\ell\right)}{(w,\ell)}{(w,\ell)}{(w,\ell)}

\displaystyle\in\operatorname{exp}_{2}^{14}(\operatorname{poly}^{97}(\ell,w)).

Theorem 1.2
.

Let
w,\ell
be integers, let
\mathchoice{\left(\mathcal{S}=\mathchoice{\left(S_{0},S_{1},\dots,S_{\ell^{\prime}}\right)}{(S_{0},S_{1},\dots,S_{\ell^{\prime}})}{(S_{0},S_{1},\dots,S_{\ell^{\prime}})}{(S_{0},S_{1},\dots,S_{\ell^{\prime}})},\mathscr{P}\right)}{(\mathcal{S}=\mathchoice{\left(S_{0},S_{1},\dots,S_{\ell^{\prime}}\right)}{(S_{0},S_{1},\dots,S_{\ell^{\prime}})}{(S_{0},S_{1},\dots,S_{\ell^{\prime}})}{(S_{0},S_{1},\dots,S_{\ell^{\prime}})},\mathscr{P})}{(\mathcal{S}=\mathchoice{\left(S_{0},S_{1},\dots,S_{\ell^{\prime}}\right)}{(S_{0},S_{1},\dots,S_{\ell^{\prime}})}{(S_{0},S_{1},\dots,S_{\ell^{\prime}})}{(S_{0},S_{1},\dots,S_{\ell^{\prime}})},\mathscr{P})}{(\mathcal{S}=\mathchoice{\left(S_{0},S_{1},\dots,S_{\ell^{\prime}}\right)}{(S_{0},S_{1},\dots,S_{\ell^{\prime}})}{(S_{0},S_{1},\dots,S_{\ell^{\prime}})}{(S_{0},S_{1},\dots,S_{\ell^{\prime}})},\mathscr{P})}
be a strict path of well-linked sets of width
w^{\prime}
and length
\ell^{\prime}
and let
\mathcal{R}
be a

-

.
If
w^{\prime}\geq{\color[rgb]{0.05,0.375,0.0375}\mathsf{w^{\prime}}_{\ref{thm:POSS_plus_back-linkage_to_COSS}}}\mathchoice{\left(w,\ell\right)}{(w,\ell)}{(w,\ell)}{(w,\ell)}
,
r\geq{\color[rgb]{0.05,0.375,0.0375}\mathsf{r}_{\ref{thm:POSS_plus_back-linkage_to_COSS}}}\mathchoice{\left(w,\ell\right)}{(w,\ell)}{(w,\ell)}{(w,\ell)}
and
\ell^{\prime}\geq{\color[rgb]{0.05,0.375,0.0375}\mathsf{\ell^{\prime}}_{\ref{thm:POSS_plus_back-linkage_to_COSS}}}\mathchoice{\left(w,\ell\right)}{(w,\ell)}{(w,\ell)}{(w,\ell)}
, then
\mathsf{D}\mathchoice{\left(\mathchoice{\left(\mathcal{S},\mathscr{P}\right)}{(\mathcal{S},\mathscr{P})}{(\mathcal{S},\mathscr{P})}{(\mathcal{S},\mathscr{P})}\cup\mathcal{R}\right)}{(\mathchoice{\left(\mathcal{S},\mathscr{P}\right)}{(\mathcal{S},\mathscr{P})}{(\mathcal{S},\mathscr{P})}{(\mathcal{S},\mathscr{P})}\cup\mathcal{R})}{(\mathchoice{\left(\mathcal{S},\mathscr{P}\right)}{(\mathcal{S},\mathscr{P})}{(\mathcal{S},\mathscr{P})}{(\mathcal{S},\mathscr{P})}\cup\mathcal{R})}{(\mathchoice{\left(\mathcal{S},\mathscr{P}\right)}{(\mathcal{S},\mathscr{P})}{(\mathcal{S},\mathscr{P})}{(\mathcal{S},\mathscr{P})}\cup\mathcal{R})}
contains a cycle of well-linked sets of width
w
and length
\ell
.

While at first it may look like the path of well-linked sets provided by
theorem

1.1
is very close to a cycle of well-linked sets, ensuring that the linkage connecting the last cluster back to the first one is internally disjoint from the path of well-linked sets is far from being a simple task, as the intersections may yield mostly short cycles which are not very helpful in building a large cylindrical grid.

Constructing such a
back-linkage
is a step in the proof of the Directed Grid Theorem which finds no parallel in the Undirected Grid Theorem, as there is no need to “close cycles” in the undirected setting.
More than just a mere technical step, a better understanding of this part of the proof can reveal further insights into the differences between the Directed and Undirected Grid Theorems.
In particular, if one could obtain linear upper bounds for this part of the proof,
then this could be evidence that the functions for the Directed and for the Undirected Grid Theorems are asymptotically the same.
Conversely, if one could obtain super-linear lower bounds, then this would be evidence that these functions might be distinct.

In order to obtain the back-linkage, we introduce the concept of
c
-
horizontal webs
, which are a special case of the webs used in
[
16
,
21
]
, and apply our framework from
[
16
]
in order to both construct a cylindrical grid from
2
-horizontal webs as well as obtaining 2-horizontal webs from the path of well-linked sets described above.

Moreover, our modular approach leads us to two questions regarding obtaining 2-horizontal webs and constructing a cylindrical grid from them (see
section

6
for an in-depth discussion), which are not only natural questions on their own, but are also both necessary and sufficient for obtaining better bounds for the Directed Grid Theorem.
In this way, we effectively reduce the complexity of further improvements of the theorem, as each question can be answered independently.

As in
[
16
]
, we first look for an intermediate structure with similar connectivity properties as the cylindrical grid, which we call
cycle of well-linked sets
.
With the tools developed in
[
16
]
, we can easily show that every large cycle of well-linked sets contains a large cylindrical grid, allowing us to deduce our main result from
theorem

1.2
.
The exact function
{\color[rgb]{0.05,0.375,0.0375}\mathsf{dtw}_{\ref{thm:high-treewidth-implies-cylindrical-grid}}}
is defined later, but we note here that
{\color[rgb]{0.05,0.375,0.0375}\mathsf{dtw}_{\ref{thm:high-treewidth-implies-cylindrical-grid}}}\mathchoice{\left(k\right)}{(k)}{(k)}{(k)}\in\operatorname{exp}_{2}^{22}(\operatorname{poly}^{9}(k))
.

Theorem 1.3
.

Every digraph
D
with
\operatorname{dtw}\mathchoice{\left(D\right)}{(D)}{(D)}{(D)}\geq{\color[rgb]{0.05,0.375,0.0375}\mathsf{dtw}_{\ref{thm:high-treewidth-implies-cylindrical-grid}}}\mathchoice{\left(k\right)}{(k)}{(k)}{(k)}
contains a cylindrical grid of order
k
as a butterfly minor.

The statement above has several implications for results using the Directed Grid Theorem.
In
section

1.1
, we analyse one such application, namely the
Erdős-Pósa property
of digraphs, and deduce that our results also improve the non-elementary bounds previously obtained by
[
3
]
to elementary ones.

The remainder of the paper is organised as follows.
Basic notation and definitions are listed in
section

2
, necessary definitions and statements from
[
16
]
are repeated in
section

3
,
section

4
contains our main proof and concluding remarks are given in
section

6
.

1.1
Erdős-Pósa property for directed graphs

One of the applications of the Directed Grid Theorem is proving the Erdős-Pósa property of digraphs.
We say that a digraph
H
has the Erdős-Pósa property if there is a function
\ell_{H}
such that, in any digraph
D
, we can find
n
disjoint
H
-butterfly minors or
\ell_{H}(n)
vertices covering all
H
-butterfly minors.
Amiri, Kawarabayashi, Kreutzer and Wollan
[
3
]
proved that the Erdős-Pósa property holds for strongly connected digraphs precisely when they are minors of a cylindrical grid.

Theorem 1.4
.

[
3
, Theorem 4.1]

Let
H
be a strongly connected digraph.
There is a function
\ell_{H}
such that the following holds.

H
has the Erdős-Pósa property for butterfly (topological) minors if, and only if, there is a cylindrical grid (wall) of order
c
of which
H
is a butterfly (topological) minor.
Furthermore, for every fixed strongly connected digraph
H
satisfying these conditions and every
k
there is a polynomial time algorithm which, given a digraph
D
as input, either computes
k
disjoint (butterfly or topological) models of
H
in
D
or a set
S
of
\leq\ell_{H}(k)
vertices such that
D-S
does not contain a model of
H
.

Their proof uses the following lemma, which we restate to make the bounds explicit.

Lemma 1.5

(
[
3
, Lemma 4.2]
)
.

Let
G
be a directed graph with
\operatorname{dtw}\mathchoice{\left(G\right)}{(G)}{(G)}{(G)}\leq w
. For each strongly connected directed graph
H
, the graph
G
has either
k
disjoint copies of
H
as a topological (butterfly) minor, or contains a set
T
of at most
k\cdot(w+1)
vertices such that
H
is not a topological (butterfly) minor of
G-T
.

The previous two results and our improved bound for the Directed Grid Theorem yield the following.

Theorem 1.6
.

Let
H
be a strongly connected digraph that is a topological
(butterfly)
minor of the cylindrical wall
(grid)
of order
c
.
Then for any digraph
D
and any natural number
k
either
D
contains
k
disjoint copies of
H
as a topological
(butterfly)
minor or a set
S
of at most
k({\color[rgb]{0.05,0.375,0.0375}\mathsf{dtw}_{\ref{statement:high-dtw-to-wall}}}\mathchoice{\left(kc\right)}{(kc)}{(kc)}{(kc)}+1)

(
k({\color[rgb]{0.05,0.375,0.0375}\mathsf{dtw}_{\ref{thm:high-treewidth-implies-cylindrical-grid}}}\mathchoice{\left(kc\right)}{(kc)}{(kc)}{(kc)}+1)
)
vertices such that
H
is not a topological
(butterfly)
minor of
D-S
.

Proof.

We consider the case where
H
is a butterfly minor of a cylindrical grid.
The other case follows analogously by applying
theorem

5.5
instead of
theorem

1.3
.

If
\operatorname{dtw}\mathchoice{\left(D\right)}{(D)}{(D)}{(D)}\geq{\color[rgb]{0.05,0.375,0.0375}\mathsf{dtw}_{\ref{thm:high-treewidth-implies-cylindrical-grid}}}\mathchoice{\left(kc\right)}{(kc)}{(kc)}{(kc)}
, then by
theorem

1.3

D
contains a cylindrical grid of order
kc
and hence
k
disjoint copies of
H
as a butterfly minor. Otherwise, we can apply
lemma

1.5
.
∎

When
H
is a cycle of length two, this result is equivalent to Younger’s conjecture, which states that for all directed graphs
D
, there exists a function
f
such that if
D
does not contain
k
disjoint cycles,
D
contains a feedback vertex set of size bounded by
f(k)
.
This conjecture was proven true in 1996 by Reed, Robertson, Seymour and Thomas, and
theorem

1.6
is an improvement of their non-elementary bound.
This bound is not the best we can achieve, and in fact, in
[
16
]
we prove that the function
f
is at most a power tower of height
8
.

2
Preliminaries

In this section, we establish our notation and recall standard concepts and results from the literature used throughout the paper.

Sequences, sets and functions.

Given sequences
S_{1}\coloneqq(x_{1},x_{2},\dots,x_{j})
and
S_{2}\coloneqq(y_{1},y_{2},\dots
,
y_{k})
, we write
S_{1}\cdot S_{2}
for the sequence
S_{3}\coloneqq(x_{1},x_{2},\dots,x_{j},y_{1},y_{2},\dots,y_{k})
.
We say that
S_{1}\cdot S_{2}
is a
decomposition
of
S_{3}
.
The following is a well-known theorem about sequences of numbers due to Erdős and Szekeres.

Theorem 2.1

(
[
10
]
)
.

Let
r,s\in\mathds{N}
.
Every sequence of distinct numbers of length at least
\mathchoice{\left(r-1\right)}{(r-1)}{(r-1)}{(r-1)}\mathchoice{\left(s-1\right)}{(s-1)}{(s-1)}{(s-1)}+1
contains a monotonically increasing subsequence of length
r
or a monotonically decreasing subsequence of length
s
.

Power towers and polynomials.

Let
d
be an integer and
V=\{x_{1},\ldots,x_{k}\}
a set of variables.
A
polynomial
of degree
d
over
V
is a function
p(x_{1},x_{2},\ldots,x_{k})
of the form
p(x_{1},x_{2},\ldots,x_{k})=\sum_{i=1}^{n}(c_{i}\prod_{j=1}^{k}x_{j}^{e_{j,i}})
, where for each
1\leq i\leq n
and each
1\leq j\leq k
we have that
c_{i}\in\mathds{R}
,
e_{j,i}\in\mathds{N}
and
\sum_{j=1}^{k}e_{j,i}\leq d
.
We write
\operatorname{poly}^{d}(x_{1},x_{2},\ldots,x_{k})
for the set of all functions
f
for which there is a polynomial
p
of degree
d
over the variable set
x_{1},x_{2},\ldots,x_{k}
such that
f(x_{1},x_{2},\ldots,x_{k})\in\mathcal{O}(p(x_{1},x_{2},\ldots,x_{k}))
.

We define
power towers
as follows.
Given an integer
h
and a set of functions
F
over a set of variables
V
, we define a set of functions
\operatorname{exp}_{2}^{h}(F)
recursively as follows.
We set
\operatorname{exp}_{2}^{0}(F)=F
and define
\operatorname{exp}_{2}^{h}(F)
as
\{f:\mathds{R}^{|V|}\to\mathds{R}\mid f\in\mathcal{O}(2^{g(V)}),g\in\operatorname{exp}_{2}^{h-1}(F)\}
for
h>1
.
If
F=\operatorname{poly}^{d}(V)
, we say that a function
f\in\operatorname{exp}_{2}^{h}(F)
is a
power tower
of height
h
.

Graphs and digraphs.

We denote by
E(G)
the edge/arc set of a graph
G
, directed or not, and by
V(G)
its vertex set.
We often use
G
for undirected graphs and
D
for directed graphs (also called
digraphs
).

Let
D
be a digraph.
Given a set of vertices
X\subseteq V\mathchoice{\left(D\right)}{(D)}{(D)}{(D)}
, we write
D-X
for the digraph
(Y\coloneqq V\mathchoice{\left(D\right)}{(D)}{(D)}{(D)}\setminus X
,
E\mathchoice{\left(D\right)}{(D)}{(D)}{(D)}\cap(Y\times Y))
.
Similarly, given a set of arcs
F\subseteq E\mathchoice{\left(D\right)}{(D)}{(D)}{(D)}
, we write
D-F
for the digraph
(V\mathchoice{\left(D\right)}{(D)}{(D)}{(D)},E\mathchoice{\left(D\right)}{(D)}{(D)}{(D)}\setminus F)
.

If
D
is a digraph and
v\in V(D)
, then
N^{\mathsf{in}}_{D}\mathchoice{\left(v\right)}{(v)}{(v)}{(v)}\coloneqq\{u\in V\mid(u,v)\in E\mathchoice{\left(D\right)}{(D)}{(D)}{(D)}\}
is the set of
in-neighbours
and
N^{\mathsf{out}}_{D}\mathchoice{\left(v\right)}{(v)}{(v)}{(v)}\coloneqq\{u\in V\mid(v,u)\in E\mathchoice{\left(D\right)}{(D)}{(D)}{(D)}\}
the set of
out-neighbours
of
v
.
By
\mathsf{indeg}_{D}(v)\coloneqq|N^{\mathsf{in}}\mathchoice{\left(v\right)}{(v)}{(v)}{(v)}|
we denote the
in-degree
of
v
and by
\mathsf{outdeg}_{D}(v)\coloneqq|N^{\mathsf{out}}\mathchoice{\left(v\right)}{(v)}{(v)}{(v)}|
its out-degree.
When working with a set or another structure
X
containing digraphs, we write
\mathsf{D}\mathchoice{\left(X\right)}{(X)}{(X)}{(X)}
to mean the digraph obtained by taking the union of all digraphs in
X
.

Paths and walks.

A
walk
of length
\ell
in a digraph
D
is a sequence of vertices
W\coloneqq\mathchoice{\left(v_{0},v_{1},\dots,v_{\ell}\right)}{(v_{0},v_{1},\dots,v_{\ell})}{(v_{0},v_{1},\dots,v_{\ell})}{(v_{0},v_{1},\dots,v_{\ell})}
such that
\mathchoice{\left(v_{i},v_{i+1}\right)}{(v_{i},v_{i+1})}{(v_{i},v_{i+1})}{(v_{i},v_{i+1})}\in E\mathchoice{\left(D\right)}{(D)}{(D)}{(D)}
, for all
0\leq i<\ell
.
We write
\mathsf{start}\mathchoice{\left(W\right)}{(W)}{(W)}{(W)}
for
v_{0}
and
\mathsf{end}\mathchoice{\left(W\right)}{(W)}{(W)}{(W)}
for
v_{\ell}
and say that
W
is a
v_{0}
-
v_{\ell}
-walk.

A walk
W\coloneqq\mathchoice{\left(v_{0},v_{1},\dots,v_{\ell}\right)}{(v_{0},v_{1},\dots,v_{\ell})}{(v_{0},v_{1},\dots,v_{\ell})}{(v_{0},v_{1},\dots,v_{\ell})}
is called a
path
if no vertex appears twice in it and it is called a
cycle
if
v_{0}=v_{\ell}
and
v_{i}\neq v_{j}
for all
0\leq i<j<\ell
.

We often identify a walk
W
in
D
with the corresponding subgraph and write
V(W)
and
E(W)
for the set of vertices and arcs appearing on it.

Given two walks
W_{1}\coloneqq(x_{1},x_{2},\dots,x_{j})
and
W_{2}\coloneqq(y_{1},y_{2},\dots,y_{k})
with
\mathsf{end}\mathchoice{\left(W_{1}\right)}{(W_{1})}{(W_{1})}{(W_{1})}=\mathsf{start}\mathchoice{\left(W_{2}\right)}{(W_{2})}{(W_{2})}{(W_{2})}
, we make use of the concatenation notation for sequences and write
W_{1}\cdot W_{2}
for the walk
W_{3}\coloneqq(x_{1},x_{2},\dots,x_{j},y_{2},y_{3},\dots,y_{k})
.
We say that
W_{1}\cdot W_{2}
is a decomposition of
W_{3}
.
If
W_{1}
or
W_{2}
is an empty sequence, then the result of
W_{1}\cdot W_{2}
is the other walk (or the empty sequence if both walks are empty).

Specific digraphs.

We denote the digraph of a path on
k
vertices by
{{\hbox{\hskip 3.99187pt\hskip-3.93053pt\hbox{$\mathbf{P}$}\hskip-3.93053pt\hskip-3.99187pt\raisebox{7.7222pt}{\hbox{$\hbox to7.98pt{\vbox to2pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 0.1507pt\lower-1.0pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {{}{{}}{}

{{}}
{
\lxSVG@setdash{0.0pt}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\lxSVG@miterjoin\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{}
{}{}{}
{}

\lxSVG@fillstroke}}
}{}{}{{}}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 0 0 L 8.76 0}{fill:none} {{}{{}}{}{}{{}}{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@transformcm{1.0}{0.0}{0.0}{1.0}{6.33235pt}{0.0pt}\lxSVG@begingroup@{transform} \lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-dasharray,stroke-dashoffset} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-linejoin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 1.4 0 C 1.23 0.07 0.47 0.45 0 0.87 L 0 -0.87 C 0.47 -0.45 1.23 -0.07 1.4 0 Z}{} \lxSVG@closescope \lxSVG@closescope }}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}}}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
\lxSVG@closescope }
\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}$}}\hskip-3.99187pt\hskip 3.99187pt}}}_{\hskip-1.20552ptk}
.
For the
bidirected path on
k
vertices, we write
{\hbox{\hskip 3.99187pt\hskip-3.93053pt\hbox{$\mathbf{P}$}\hskip-3.93053pt\hskip-3.99187pt\raisebox{7.7222pt}{\hbox{$\hbox to7.98pt{\vbox to2pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 0.1507pt\lower-1.0pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {{}{{}}{}

\lxSVG@closescope }
\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}$}}\hskip-3.99187pt\hskip 3.99187pt}}_{\hskip-1.20552ptk}\coloneqq(\{u_{1},u_{2},\dots,u_{k}\},\{(u_{i},u_{j})\mid 1\leq i,j\leq k\text{ and }|i-j|=1\})
.
The
cycle on
k
vertices
is given by
{\hbox{\hskip 4.15276pt\hskip-4.15276pt\hbox{$\mathbf{C}$}\hskip-4.15276pt\hskip-3.99187pt\raisebox{7.7222pt}{\hbox{$\hbox to7.98pt{\vbox to2pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 0.1507pt\lower-1.0pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {{}{{}}{}
{}{}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{}{}{}{}{{}}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 0 0 L 8.76 0}{fill:none} {{}{{}}{}{}{{}}{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@transformcm{1.0}{0.0}{0.0}{1.0}{6.33235pt}{0.0pt}\lxSVG@begingroup@{transform} \lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-dasharray,stroke-dashoffset} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-linejoin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 1.4 0 C 1.23 0.07 0.47 0.45 0 0.87 L 0 -0.87 C 0.47 -0.45 1.23 -0.07 1.4 0 Z}{} \lxSVG@closescope \lxSVG@closescope }}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}}}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
\lxSVG@closescope }
\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}$}}\hskip-3.99187pt\hskip 4.15276pt}}_{\hskip-0.3014ptk}\coloneqq(\{u_{0},u_{1},\dots,u_{k-1}\},\{(u_{i},u_{i+1\mod k})\mid 0\leq i<k\})
.

Connectivity.

A digraph
D
is said to be
strongly connected
if for every
u,v\in V
there is a
u
-
v
-path
and
a
v
-
u
-path in
D
.
We say
D
is
unilateral
if for every
u,v\in V
there is a
u
-
v
-path
or
a
v
-
u
-path in
D
.

Linkages and separators.

Let
A,B\subseteq V(D)
.
An
A
-
B
-walk is a walk
W
that starts in
A
and ends in
B
.
A set
X\subseteq V\mathchoice{\left(D\right)}{(D)}{(D)}{(D)}
is an
A
-
B
-separator
if there are no
A
-
B
-paths in
D-X
.

A
linkage
in
D
is a set
\mathcal{L}
of pairwise vertex-disjoint paths.
The
order

|\mathcal{L}|
of
\mathcal{L}
is the number of paths it contains.

Given a linkage
\mathcal{L}
and a digraph
H
, we say that
\mathcal{L}
is
internally disjoint
from
H
if
V\mathchoice{\left(\mathcal{L}\right)}{(\mathcal{L})}{(\mathcal{L})}{(\mathcal{L})}\cap V\mathchoice{\left(H\right)}{(H)}{(H)}{(H)}\subseteq\mathsf{start}\mathchoice{\left(\mathcal{L}\right)}{(\mathcal{L})}{(\mathcal{L})}{(\mathcal{L})}\cup\mathsf{end}\mathchoice{\left(\mathcal{L}\right)}{(\mathcal{L})}{(\mathcal{L})}{(\mathcal{L})}
.

An
A
-
B
-linkage
of order
k
is a linkage
\mathcal{L}\coloneqq\{L_{1},L_{2},\dots,L_{k}\}
such that
\mathsf{start}\mathchoice{\left(L_{i}\right)}{(L_{i})}{(L_{i})}{(L_{i})}\in A
and
\mathsf{end}\mathchoice{\left(L_{i}\right)}{(L_{i})}{(L_{i})}{(L_{i})}\in B
for all
1\leq i\leq k
.
We write
\mathsf{start}\mathchoice{\left(\mathcal{L}\right)}{(\mathcal{L})}{(\mathcal{L})}{(\mathcal{L})}
for the set
\{\mathsf{start}\mathchoice{\left(L_{i}\right)}{(L_{i})}{(L_{i})}{(L_{i})}\mid L_{i}\in\mathcal{L}\}
and
\mathsf{end}\mathchoice{\left(\mathcal{L}\right)}{(\mathcal{L})}{(\mathcal{L})}{(\mathcal{L})}
for the set
\{\mathsf{end}\mathchoice{\left(L_{i}\right)}{(L_{i})}{(L_{i})}{(L_{i})}\mid L_{i}\in\mathcal{L}\}
.
We also extend the notation for path concatenation to linkages.
Given linkages
\mathcal{P}=\{P_{1},P_{2},\dots,P_{k}\}
and
\mathcal{Q}=\{Q_{1},Q_{2},\dots,Q_{k}\}
such that
\mathsf{end}\mathchoice{\left(\mathcal{P}\right)}{(\mathcal{P})}{(\mathcal{P})}{(\mathcal{P})}=\mathsf{start}\mathchoice{\left(\mathcal{Q}\right)}{(\mathcal{Q})}{(\mathcal{Q})}{(\mathcal{Q})}
, we write
\mathcal{P}\cdot\mathcal{Q}
for the linkage
\{P_{a}\cdot Q_{b}\mid P_{a}\in\mathcal{P},Q_{b}\in\mathcal{Q}\text{ and }\mathsf{end}\mathchoice{\left(P_{a}\right)}{(P_{a})}{(P_{a})}{(P_{a})}=\mathsf{start}\mathchoice{\left(Q_{b}\right)}{(Q_{b})}{(Q_{b})}{(Q_{b})}\}
.

It is often convenient to use a linkage
\mathcal{L}
as a function
\mathcal{L}:\mathsf{start}\mathchoice{\left(\mathcal{L}\right)}{(\mathcal{L})}{(\mathcal{L})}{(\mathcal{L})}\to\mathsf{end}\mathchoice{\left(\mathcal{L}\right)}{(\mathcal{L})}{(\mathcal{L})}{(\mathcal{L})}
.
The expression
\mathcal{L}\mathchoice{\left(a\right)}{(a)}{(a)}{(a)}=b
then means that
\mathcal{L}
contains a path starting in
a
and ending in
b
.

We frequently use the following classical result by Menger
[
25
]
.

Theorem 2.2

(Menger’s Theorem
[
25
]
)
.

Let
D
be a digraph,
A,B\subseteq V\mathchoice{\left(D\right)}{(D)}{(D)}{(D)}
with
|A|=|B|
.
There is an
A
-
B
-linkage of size
k
in
D
if and only if every
A
-
B
-separator has size at least
k
.

Let
D
be a digraph,
A,B\subseteq V(D)
and
c\in\mathds{N}
.
An
A
-
B
-linkage with congestion
c
in
D
is a set
\mathcal{L}
of
A
-
B
-paths such that no vertex of
V(D)
occurs in more than
c
distinct paths in
\mathcal{L}
.
A linkage of congestion
1
is called
integral
, and a linkage of congestion
2
is called
half-integral
.

A simple application of
theorem

2.2
yields the following lemma (see, for example,
[
21
]
for a proof).

Lemma 2.3

(
[
21
]
)
.

Let
D
be a digraph,
A,B\subseteq V\mathchoice{\left(D\right)}{(D)}{(D)}{(D)}
.
If there is an
A
-
B
-linkage of order
ck
and congestion
c
in
D
, then there is an integral
A
-
B
-linkage of order
k
in
D
.

Throughout the paper, we frequently work with a special kind of linkage which we define next.

Definition 2.1

(minimal linkage)
.

Let
D
be a digraph,
H\subseteq D
be a subgraph, and
\mathcal{L}
be a linkage of order
k
.

\mathcal{L}
is
minimal with respect to
H
, or
H
-minimal
, if for all arcs
e\in\bigcup_{L\in\mathcal{L}}E\mathchoice{\left(L\right)}{(L)}{(L)}{(L)}\setminus E\mathchoice{\left(H\right)}{(H)}{(H)}{(H)}
there is no
\mathsf{start}\mathchoice{\left(\mathcal{L}\right)}{(\mathcal{L})}{(\mathcal{L})}{(\mathcal{L})}
-
\mathsf{end}\mathchoice{\left(\mathcal{L}\right)}{(\mathcal{L})}{(\mathcal{L})}{(\mathcal{L})}
-linkage of order
k
in the graph
(\mathcal{L}\cup H)-e
.

Given a linkage
\mathcal{L}
in a digraph
D
and a subgraph
H\subseteq D
, we can obtain a linkage
\mathcal{L}^{\prime}
with the same order and same endpoints as
\mathcal{L}
which is
H
-minimal.
This can be done by iteratively removing arcs
e\in E\mathchoice{\left(\mathcal{L}\right)}{(\mathcal{L})}{(\mathcal{L})}{(\mathcal{L})}\setminus E\mathchoice{\left(H\right)}{(H)}{(H)}{(H)}
for which a
\mathsf{start}\mathchoice{\left(\mathcal{L}\right)}{(\mathcal{L})}{(\mathcal{L})}{(\mathcal{L})}
-
\mathsf{end}\mathchoice{\left(\mathcal{L}\right)}{(\mathcal{L})}{(\mathcal{L})}{(\mathcal{L})}
-linkage of order
|\mathcal{L}|
avoiding
e
exists.

Minimal linkages were used extensively in
[
21
]
.
The idea is that, when constructing paths of an
H
-minimal linkage
\mathcal{L}
, we always prefer to use arcs of
H
over arcs not in
E(H)
.
This implies the following property, which we exploit frequently in our proofs.

Definition 2.2

(weak minimality)
.

A linkage
\mathcal{L}
in a digraph
D
is
weakly
k
-minimal
with respect to a subgraph
H
of
D
if for every
L_{1}\cdot e\cdot L_{2}\in\mathcal{L}
with
e\in E\mathchoice{\left(\mathcal{L}\right)}{(\mathcal{L})}{(\mathcal{L})}{(\mathcal{L})}\setminus E\mathchoice{\left(H\right)}{(H)}{(H)}{(H)}
there is a
V\mathchoice{\left(L_{1}\right)}{(L_{1})}{(L_{1})}{(L_{1})}
-
V\mathchoice{\left(L_{2}\right)}{(L_{2})}{(L_{2})}{(L_{2})}
-separator of size at most
k-1
in
\mathchoice{\left(\mathcal{L}\cup H\right)}{(\mathcal{L}\cup H)}{(\mathcal{L}\cup H)}{(\mathcal{L}\cup H)}-e
.

Observation 2.4
.

Let
H
be a subgraph of a digraph
D
and let
\mathcal{L}
be a linkage which is
H
-minimal.
Then
\mathcal{L}
is weakly
|\mathcal{L}|
-minimal with respect to
H
.

Proof 2.3
.

Assume towards a contradiction that there is some
L\in\mathcal{L}
and some
e\in E\mathchoice{\left(L\right)}{(L)}{(L)}{(L)}\setminus E\mathchoice{\left(H\right)}{(H)}{(H)}{(H)}
such that
L
can be decomposed into
L_{1}\cdot e\cdot L_{2}
and there is no
V\mathchoice{\left(L_{1}\right)}{(L_{1})}{(L_{1})}{(L_{1})}
-
V\mathchoice{\left(L_{2}\right)}{(L_{2})}{(L_{2})}{(L_{2})}
-separator of size less than
|\mathcal{L}|
in
\mathsf{D}\mathchoice{\left(\mathcal{L}\cup H\right)}{(\mathcal{L}\cup H)}{(\mathcal{L}\cup H)}{(\mathcal{L}\cup H)}-e
.
By
theorem

2.2
, there is a
V\mathchoice{\left(L_{1}\right)}{(L_{1})}{(L_{1})}{(L_{1})}
-
V\mathchoice{\left(L_{2}\right)}{(L_{2})}{(L_{2})}{(L_{2})}
-linkage
\mathcal{Q}
of order
|\mathcal{L}|
in
\mathsf{D}\mathchoice{\left(\mathcal{L}\cup H\right)}{(\mathcal{L}\cup H)}{(\mathcal{L}\cup H)}{(\mathcal{L}\cup H)}-e
.

Let
S
be a minimum
\mathsf{start}\mathchoice{\left(\mathcal{L}\right)}{(\mathcal{L})}{(\mathcal{L})}{(\mathcal{L})}
-
\mathsf{end}\mathchoice{\left(\mathcal{L}\right)}{(\mathcal{L})}{(\mathcal{L})}{(\mathcal{L})}
-separator in
\mathsf{D}\mathchoice{\left(\mathcal{L}\cup H\right)}{(\mathcal{L}\cup H)}{(\mathcal{L}\cup H)}{(\mathcal{L}\cup H)}-e
.
Because
\mathcal{L}
is
H
-minimal, we have that
|S|<|\mathcal{L}|
.
Hence,
S
must hit every path in
\mathcal{L}\setminus\{L\}
and must be disjoint from
L
.

Since
|\mathcal{Q}|=|\mathcal{L}|
, there is some
Q\in\mathcal{Q}
which is not hit by
S
.
Hence, there is a
\mathsf{start}\mathchoice{\left(L\right)}{(L)}{(L)}{(L)}
-
\mathsf{end}\mathchoice{\left(L\right)}{(L)}{(L)}{(L)}
-path in
\mathsf{D}\mathchoice{\left(\mathcal{L}\cup H\right)}{(\mathcal{L}\cup H)}{(\mathcal{L}\cup H)}{(\mathcal{L}\cup H)}-e-S
, a contradiction to the assumption that
S
is a separator.
Thus,
\mathcal{L}
is weakly
|\mathcal{L}|
-minimal with respect to
H
.

We close this part by recalling the definition of well-linkedness, an important property of a central concept in our proof, the path of well-linked sets.

Definition 2.4
.

Let
A,B
be sets of vertices in a digraph
D
.
We say that
A
is well-linked to
B
in
D
if for every
A^{\prime}\subseteq A
and every
B^{\prime}\subseteq B
with
|A^{\prime}|=|B^{\prime}|
there is an
A^{\prime}
-
B^{\prime}
-linkage of order
|A^{\prime}|
in
D
.
If
A
is well-linked to
A
, then we say that
A
is a
well-linked set
.

Minors.

Given a digraph
D
and an arc
(u,v)\in E\mathchoice{\left(D\right)}{(D)}{(D)}{(D)}
, we say that
(u,v)
is
butterfly contractible
if
\mathsf{outdeg}(u)=1
or
\mathsf{indeg}(v)=1
.
The
butterfly contraction
of
(u,v)
is the operation which consists in removing
u
and
v
from
D
, then adding a new vertex
uv
, together with the arcs
\{(w,uv)\mid w\in N^{\mathsf{in}}_{D}\mathchoice{\left(u\right)}{(u)}{(u)}{(u)}\}
and
\{(uv,w)\mid w\in N^{\mathsf{out}}_{D}\mathchoice{\left(v\right)}{(v)}{(v)}{(v)}\}
.
Note that, by definition of digraphs, we
remove
duplicated arcs and loops, that is, arcs of the form
(w,w)
.
If there is a subgraph
D^{\prime}
of
D
such that we can construct another digraph
H
from
D^{\prime}
using butterfly contractions, then we say that
H
is a
butterfly minor of
D
, or that
D
contains
H
as a butterfly minor
.

Definition 2.5
.

Let
H
and
D
be directed graphs.
A
butterfly-model
of
H
in
D
is a function
\mu
which assigns to every
x\in V(H)\cup E\mathchoice{\left(H\right)}{(H)}{(H)}{(H)}
a subdigraph of
D

such that:

1.

for every pair of distinct vertices
u,v\in V(H)
,
\mu(u)
and
\mu(v)
are vertex-disjoint,

2.

for a vertex
v\in V(H)
and a non-incident edge
e\in E\mathchoice{\left(H\right)}{(H)}{(H)}{(H)}
,
\mu(v)
and
\mu(e)
are vertex-disjoint,

3.

for every
v\in V(H)
,
\mu(v)
is the union of an in-tree and an out-tree intersecting exactly on their common root, and

4.

for every
(u,v)\in E\mathchoice{\left(H\right)}{(H)}{(H)}{(H)}
,
\mu((u,v))
is a directed path starting at a vertex of the out-tree of
\mu(u)
and ending at a vertex of the in-tree of
\mu(v)
.

Given a digraph
D
and an arc
(u,v)\in E\mathchoice{\left(D\right)}{(D)}{(D)}{(D)}
, a
subdivision
of
(u,v)
is the operation consisting in removing
(u,v)
, adding a new vertex
w
and two new arcs

(u,w),(w,u)
to
D
.
A subdivision of
D
is any digraph obtained after subdividing any number of arcs of
D
.
We say that a digraph
H
is a
topological minor
of
D
if some subdivision of
H
is contained as a subgraph in
D
.

The following statement is well known.
We include a proof here for completeness.

Observation 2.5
.

Let
D,H
be digraphs.
If
\mathsf{indeg}(u)\leq 2
,
\mathsf{outdeg}(u)\leq 2
and
\mathsf{indeg}(v)+\mathsf{outdeg}(v)\leq 3
holds for every vertex
v
of
H

and
D
contains
H
as a butterfly minor,
then
D
also contains
H
as a topological minor.

Proof 2.6
.

Let
u_{1},u_{2},\ldots,u_{n}
be the vertices of
H
.
Let
\mu
be a butterfly model of
H
in
D
.
We find distinct vertices
v_{1},v_{2},\ldots,v_{n}
in
D
as follows.

If
u_{i}
has in-degree two, then it must have out-degree at most one.
Let
w_{1},w_{2}
be the two in-neighbours of
u_{i}
in
H
,
let
L_{1}=\mu((w_{1},u_{i}))
and
let
L_{2}=\mu((w_{2},u_{i}))
.
Let
c_{i}
be a vertex of
\mu(u_{i})

which is reachable from all sources of
\mu(u_{i})
and
can reach all sinks of
\mu(u_{i})
.

By definition of
\mu
, there are two paths
P_{1},P_{2}
from

\mathsf{end}\mathchoice{\left(L_{1}\right)}{(L_{1})}{(L_{1})}{(L_{1})}
and
\mathsf{end}\mathchoice{\left(L_{2}\right)}{(L_{2})}{(L_{2})}{(L_{2})}
to
c_{i}
, respectively.
Choose
v_{i}
as the first vertex in the intersection between
P_{1}
and
P_{2}
.

If
u_{i}
has out-degree two, we proceed in an analogous fashion as above,
taking
w_{1},w_{2}
as the out-neighbours of
u_{i}
instead.

By definition of
\mu
, there are two paths
P_{1},P_{2}
from

c_{i}
to
\mathsf{start}\mathchoice{\left(L_{1}\right)}{(L_{1})}{(L_{1})}{(L_{1})}
and
\mathsf{start}\mathchoice{\left(L_{2}\right)}{(L_{2})}{(L_{2})}{(L_{2})}
, respectively.
Choose
v_{i}
as the last vertex in the intersection between
P_{1}
and
P_{2}
.

If
u_{i}
has both in-degree and out-degree at most one, we choose
v_{i}=c_{i}
.

We now construct a subdivision
H^{\prime}
of
H
.
For every arc
(u_{i},u_{j})∈E\mathchoice{\left(H\right)}{(H)}{(H)}{(H)}
,
let
P_{i,j}
be a path from
v_{i}
to
v_{j}
inside the union of

\mu(u_{i})
,
\mu(u_{j})
and
\mu((u_{i},u_{j}))

containing
\mu((u_{i},u_{j}))
as a subpath.
We subdivide the arc
(u_{i},u_{j})

|V\mathchoice{\left(P_{i,j}\right)}{(P_{i,j})}{(P_{i,j})}{(P_{i,j})}|-2
many times.
By choice of
v_{i}
and because
u_{i}
has in-degree or out-degree at most 2, any two distinct paths
P_{i,j},P_{x,y}
are internally disjoint.
Hence,
D
contains
H^{\prime}
as a subdigraph, and, thus,
H
as a topological minor.

2.1
Directed treewidth and cylindrical grids

In this
section
, we recall the definition of directed treewidth and the concepts of webs, cylindrical grids and cylindrical walls.

Directed treewidth was originally introduced by Reed
[
28
]
and by Johnson, Robertson, Seymour and Thomas
[
19
]
(see also
[
18
]
).
Adler
[
2
]
showed that the original definition in
[
19
]
of directed treewidth is not closed under butterfly minors.
We, therefore, use the variant of directed treewidth defined in
[
22
]
, which is closed under taking butterfly minors
[
23
]
.

However, we only include this definition for the sake of completeness, as our proofs do not immediately use it.

An
arborescence

T
is an acyclic directed graph obtained from an undirected rooted tree by orienting all edges away from the root.
That is,
T
has a vertex
r_{0}
, called the root of
T
, with the property that for every
r\in V(T)
there is a unique directed path from
r_{0}
to
r
in
T
.
For each
r\in V(T)
, we denote the subarborescence of
T
induced by the set of vertices in
T
reachable from
r
by
T_{r}
.
In particular,
r
is the root of
T_{r}
.

Definition 2.7

(
[
22
, Definition 3.1]
)
.

A
directed tree-decomposition
of a digraph
D
is a triple
(T,\beta,\gamma)
, where
\beta:V(T)\to 2^{V(D)}
and
\gamma:E(T)\to 2^{V(D)}
are functions and
T
is an arborescence such that

(T1)

\{\beta(t):t\in V(T)\}
is a partition of
V(D)
into (possibly empty) sets and

(T2)

for every
e=(s,t)\in E(T)
, there is no closed directed walk in
D-\gamma(e)
containing a vertex in
A
and a vertex in
B
, where
A=\bigcup\{\beta(t):t\in V(T_{t})\}
and
B=V(D)\setminus A
.

For
t\in V(T)
we define
\Gamma(t)\coloneqq\beta(t)\cup\bigcup\{\gamma(e):e\sim t\}
, where
e\sim t
if
e
is incident to
t
, and we define
\beta(T_{t})\coloneqq\bigcup\{\beta(t):t\in V(T_{t})\}
.
The
width
of
(T,\beta,\gamma)
is the smallest integer
w
such that
|\Gamma(t)|\leq w+1
for all
t\in V(T)
.
The
directed treewidth
of
D
is the smallest integer
w
such that
D
has a directed tree-decomposition of width
w
.
The sets
\beta(t)
are called the bags and the sets
\gamma(e)
are called the guards of the directed tree-decomposition.

We now define another obstruction to directed treewidth called
cylindrical grids
,
which are illustrated in
fig.

1
.
Since we are interested in grids in the context of butterfly minors, we define grids by linkages instead of giving explicit vertex and arc sets.

Definition 2.8
.

A
cylindrical grid
of order
k
is a digraph
G_{k}
consisting of
k
pairwise disjoint directed cycles
C_{1},C_{2},\dots,C_{k}
of length
2k
, together with a set of
2k
pairwise vertex-disjoint paths
P_{1},P_{2},\dots,P_{2k}
of length
k-1
such that

\blacktriangleright

each path
P_{i}
has exactly one vertex in common with each cycle
C_{j}
and both endpoints of
P_{i}
are in
V\mathchoice{\left(C_{1}\right)}{(C_{1})}{(C_{1})}{(C_{1})}\cup V\mathchoice{\left(C_{k}\right)}{(C_{k})}{(C_{k})}{(C_{k})}
,

\blacktriangleright

the paths
P_{1},P_{2},\dots,P_{2k}
appear on each
C_{i}
in this order, and

\blacktriangleright

for each
1\leq i\leq 2k
, if
i
is odd, then the cycles
C_{1},C_{2},\dots,C_{k}
occur on
P_{i}
in this order and, if
i
is even, then the cycles occur in the reverse order
C_{k},C_{k-1},\dots,C_{1}
.

Figure 1
:
A cylindrical grid of order 3 on the left and the cylindrical wall of order 3 on the right.
The split vertices on the wall have a grey background,
where the cyan pentagon is the vertex
v^{\text{out}}
and
the blue rectangle is the vertex
v^{\text{in}}
.

Working with butterfly minors can sometimes require technical proofs due to the complexity of butterfly minor models.
For this reason, stating our results in terms of topological minors is also desirable.
To do so, we need a slightly different definition of a cylindrical grid, called a
cylindrical wall
.
In the context of topological minors, it is more convenient to define the cylindrical wall of order
k
as a single digraph and not as a family of digraphs.

A
cylindrical wall of order
k
is obtained from the cylindrical grid of order
k
with a minimum number of vertices by splitting every vertex of in-degree and out-degree two in the following way.
Replace a vertex
v
by two vertices
v^{\text{in}},v^{\text{out}}
, add the arc
(v^{\text{in}},v^{\text{out}})
, replace every arc
(u,v)
with the arc
(u,v^{\text{in}})
and every arc
(v,u)
with the arc
(v^{\text{out}},u)
.

It is a simple exercise to see that every cylindrical grid of order
2k-2

contains the cylindrical wall of order
k
.

Figure 2
:
How a subdivision of the cylindrical wall of order 3 can be constructed inside a cylindrical grid of order 4.
Unused arcs and vertices of the grid are coloured grey.
The split vertices of the wall have a grey background,
where the blue square vertex represents
v^{\text{in}}
and
the cyan pentagon vertex represents
v^{\text{out}}
.

Observation 2.6
.

Every cylindrical grid of order
2k-2
contains the cylindrical wall of order
k
as a topological minor.

Proof 2.9
.

Let
P_{1},P_{2},\ldots,P_{4k-4}
be the paths and

C_{1},C_{2},\ldots,C_{2k-2}
be the cycles of the given cylindrical grid
as described in
definition

2.8
.
We construct a subdivision of the cylindrical wall of order
k
as follows.

Define
C^{\prime}_{1}=C_{1}
and
C^{\prime}_{k}=C_{2k-2}
.
For each
1\leq j\leq k
,
let
v^{\text{out}}_{1,2j-1}
be the vertex of
P_{2j-1}
in
C_{1}
,
let
v^{\text{in}}_{1,2j}
be the vertex of
P_{2j}
in
C_{1}
,
let
v^{\text{in}}_{k,2j-1}
be the vertex of
P_{2j-1}
in
C_{2k-2}
and
let
v^{\text{out}}_{k,2j}
be the vertex of
P_{2j}
in
C_{2k-2}
.

For each
2\leq i\leq k-1
, construct
C^{\prime}_{i}
as follows.
For each
1\leq j\leq 2k
,
let
\mathsf{a}=\mathsf{in},\mathsf{b}=\mathsf{out}
if
j
is odd and
let
\mathsf{a}=\mathsf{out},\mathsf{b}=\mathsf{in}
if
j
is even.
Let
v^{\mathsf{a}}_{i,j}
be the vertex of
P_{j}
on
C_{2(i-1)}
and
let
v^{\mathsf{b}}_{i,j}
be the vertex of
P_{j}
on
C_{2(i-1)+1}
.
Let
L_{i,j}
be the

v^{\mathsf{out}}_{i,j}
-
v^{\mathsf{in}}_{i,j+1}
-subpath
of
C_{2(i-1)}
(if
j
is even) or

C_{2(i-1)+1}
(if
j
is odd).
Let
R_{i}
be the
v^{\mathsf{out}}_{i,2k}
-
v^{\mathsf{in}}_{i,1}
-subpath
of
C_{2(i-1)}
.
Define
C^{\prime}_{i}=L_{i,1}\cdot L_{i,2}\cdot\ldots\cdot L_{i,2k}\cdot R_{i}
.

For each
1\leq i\leq k-1
and
each
1\leq j\leq 2k
,
define the paths
A_{i,j},B_{i,j}
as follows.

B_{i,j}
is the
v^{\mathsf{in}}_{i,j}
-
v^{\mathsf{out}}_{i,j}
-subpath of
P_{j}
.
If
j
is even,
define
A_{i,j}
as the

v^{\mathsf{out}}_{i,j}
-
v^{\mathsf{in}}_{i+1,j}
-subpath of
P_{j}
.
If
j
is odd,
define
A_{i,j}
as the

v^{\mathsf{out}}_{i+1,j}
-
v^{\mathsf{in}}_{i,j}
-subpath of
P_{j}
.

One can confirm that the union of all
C^{\prime}_{i},A_{i,j},B_{i,j}
defined above
is a subdivision of the cylindrical wall of order
k
, completing the proof.

An essential difference between cylindrical grids and grids in undirected graphs is that cylindrical grids are
locally acyclic
in the following sense.
Suppose we delete in each cycle
C_{i}
the arc
e_{i}
whose head is on the path
P_{1}
.
These arcs are drawn in
orange
in
fig.

1
.
The resulting digraph is acyclic and consists of two linkages: the linkage
\{P_{1},\dots,P_{2k}\}
and the linkage
\{C_{1}-e_{1},\dots,C_{k}-e_{k}\}
which contains for each cycle
C_{i}
the path that remains once the arc
e_{i}
is deleted.
Digraphs of this form are called
fences
.
See
fig.

3(b)
for a drawing of cylindrical grids illustrating how they are constructed from a fence with additional arcs closing the cycles.

Definition 2.10
.

A
(p,q)
-fence
is a tuple
(\mathcal{P},\mathcal{Q})
such that

\blacktriangleright

\mathcal{P}=\mathchoice{\left(P_{1},P_{2},\dots,P_{2p}\right)}{(P_{1},P_{2},\dots,P_{2p})}{(P_{1},P_{2},\dots,P_{2p})}{(P_{1},P_{2},\dots,P_{2p})}
and
\mathcal{Q}=\mathchoice{\left(Q_{1},Q_{2},\dots,Q_{q}\right)}{(Q_{1},Q_{2},\dots,Q_{q})}{(Q_{1},Q_{2},\dots,Q_{q})}{(Q_{1},Q_{2},\dots,Q_{q})}
are linkages,

\blacktriangleright

for each
1\leq i\leq 2p
and each
1\leq j\leq q
, the digraph
P_{i}\cap Q_{j}
is a path (and therefore non-empty),

\blacktriangleright

for each
1\leq j\leq q
, the paths
P_{1}\cap Q_{j},P_{2}\cap Q_{j},\dots,P_{2p}\cap Q_{j}
appear in this order along
Q_{j}
, and

\blacktriangleright

for each
1\leq i\leq 2p
, if
i
is odd then the paths
P_{i}\cap Q_{1},P_{i}\cap Q_{2},\dots,P_{i}\cap Q_{q}
appear in this order along
P_{i}
, and if
i
is even instead, then the paths
P_{i}\cap Q_{q},P_{i}\cap Q_{q-1},\dots,P_{i}\cap Q_{1}
appear in this order along
P_{i}
.

See
fig.

3(a)
for an illustration.
The “horizontal” paths, or
rows
, constitute the linkage
\mathcal{Q}
and the
columns
form the linkage
\mathcal{P}
.

(a)

An
(8,8)
-fence.

(b)

A cylindrical grid of order
8
.

Figure 3
:
A fence and, on the right, an illustration of how a cylindrical grid is obtained from a fence.
The
dotted orange
paths symbolise the arcs
e_{i}
that close the cycles drawn solid in
fig.

1
.

Further decomposing the fence constructed from the cylindrical grid yields an even simpler form of directed grid.
In a fence, we can only route from “left to right” and we can route “upwards” as well as “downwards”.
An even simpler form of a directed grid is obtained if we remove the “upwards” paths from a fence, that is, every second column.
The resulting digraph is called an
acyclic grid
, illustrated in
fig.

4(a)
.

(a)

An acyclic
(8,8)
-grid.

(b)

An
(8,8)
-fence.

Figure 4
:
An acyclic grid and a fence.

Definition 2.11
.

An
acyclic
(p,q)
-grid
is a pair
(\mathcal{P},\mathcal{Q})
such that

\blacktriangleright

\mathcal{P}=\mathchoice{\left(P_{1},P_{2},\dots,P_{p}\right)}{(P_{1},P_{2},\dots,P_{p})}{(P_{1},P_{2},\dots,P_{p})}{(P_{1},P_{2},\dots,P_{p})}
and
\mathcal{Q}=\mathchoice{\left(Q_{1},Q_{2},\dots,Q_{q}\right)}{(Q_{1},Q_{2},\dots,Q_{q})}{(Q_{1},Q_{2},\dots,Q_{q})}{(Q_{1},Q_{2},\dots,Q_{q})}
are linkages,

\blacktriangleright

for each
1\leq i\leq p
and each
1\leq j\leq q
, the digraph
P_{i}\cap Q_{j}
is a path (and therefore non-empty),

\blacktriangleright

for each
1\leq j\leq q
, the paths
P_{1}\cap Q_{j},P_{2}\cap Q_{j},\dots,P_{p}\cap Q_{j}
appear in this order along
Q_{j}
, and

\blacktriangleright

for each
1\leq i\leq p
, the paths
P_{i}\cap Q_{1},P_{i}\cap Q_{2},\dots,P_{i}\cap Q_{q}
appear in this order along
P_{i}
.

Another grid-like structure we define is the
web
, initially introduced by Reed et al. in
[
27
]
.
Webs are essential in the proof of the Directed Grid Theorem in
[
21
]
and are equally crucial for our results.

Definition 2.12
.

Let
D
be a digraph.
Two linkages
\mathcal{H}
and
\mathcal{V}
in
D
constitute an
\mathchoice{\left(|\mathcal{H}|,|\mathcal{V}|\right)}{(|\mathcal{H}|,|\mathcal{V}|)}{(|\mathcal{H}|,|\mathcal{V}|)}{(|\mathcal{H}|,|\mathcal{V}|)}
-web
\mathchoice{\left(\mathcal{H},\mathcal{V}\right)}{(\mathcal{H},\mathcal{V})}{(\mathcal{H},\mathcal{V})}{(\mathcal{H},\mathcal{V})}
if every path in
\mathcal{V}
intersects every path in
\mathcal{H}
.

The set
\mathsf{start}\mathchoice{\left(\mathcal{V}\right)}{(\mathcal{V})}{(\mathcal{V})}{(\mathcal{V})}
is called the
top
of the web, while the set
\mathsf{end}\mathchoice{\left(\mathcal{V}\right)}{(\mathcal{V})}{(\mathcal{V})}{(\mathcal{V})}
is called the
bottom
of the web.
Finally,
\mathchoice{\left(\mathcal{H},\mathcal{V}\right)}{(\mathcal{H},\mathcal{V})}{(\mathcal{H},\mathcal{V})}{(\mathcal{H},\mathcal{V})}
is
well-linked
if
\mathsf{end}\mathchoice{\left(\mathcal{V}\right)}{(\mathcal{V})}{(\mathcal{V})}{(\mathcal{V})}
is well-linked linked to
\mathsf{start}\mathchoice{\left(\mathcal{V}\right)}{(\mathcal{V})}{(\mathcal{V})}{(\mathcal{V})}
in
D
.

Next to these webs, we use the following two more restrictive definitions.

Definition 2.13
.

Let
\mathchoice{\left(\mathcal{H},\mathcal{V}\right)}{(\mathcal{H},\mathcal{V})}{(\mathcal{H},\mathcal{V})}{(\mathcal{H},\mathcal{V})}
be an
\mathchoice{\left(h,v\right)}{(h,v)}{(h,v)}{(h,v)}
-web.
We say that
\mathchoice{\left(\mathcal{H},\mathcal{V}\right)}{(\mathcal{H},\mathcal{V})}{(\mathcal{H},\mathcal{V})}{(\mathcal{H},\mathcal{V})}
is an
ordered web
if there is an ordering of
\mathcal{V}=\mathchoice{\left(V_{1},V_{2},\dots,V_{v}\right)}{(V_{1},V_{2},\dots,V_{v})}{(V_{1},V_{2},\dots,V_{v})}{(V_{1},V_{2},\dots,V_{v})}
for which each path
H\in\mathcal{H}
can be decomposed into
H=H_{1}\cdot H_{2}\cdot\ldots\cdot H_{v}
such that
H_{i}
intersects
V_{j}
if and only if
i=j
.

Definition 2.14
.

An
(h,v)
-web
\mathchoice{\left(\mathcal{H},\mathcal{V}\right)}{(\mathcal{H},\mathcal{V})}{(\mathcal{H},\mathcal{V})}{(\mathcal{H},\mathcal{V})}
is a
folded web
if every
V_{i}\in\mathcal{V}
can be split as
V_{i}^{a}\cdot V_{i}^{b}\coloneqq V_{i}
such that both
V_{i}^{a}
and
V_{i}^{b}
intersect all paths of
\mathcal{H}
.

3
Path of sets and temporal digraphs

In
[
16
]
, we introduced the concept of paths of well-linked and order-linked sets.
Here, we repeat some of the definitions and statements we use.
First, we need a concept similar to well-linkedness, but which describes the connectivity given by an acyclic grid.
This property is called
order-linkedness
, defined below.

Shifts and order-linkedness.

Let
A=(a_{1},\dots,a_{n})
and
B=(b_{1},\dots,b_{m})
be ordered sets.
Let
r\in\mathds{N},
let
A^{\prime}
be an ordered subset of
A
and let
B^{\prime}
be an ordered subset of
B
such that
|A^{\prime}|=|B^{\prime}|.

We say that
B^{\prime}
is an
r
-shift of
A^{\prime}
if there is a bijection
\pi:A^{\prime}\rightarrow B^{\prime}
such that

\blacktriangleright

for all
a_{i}\in A^{\prime}
we have that
\pi(a_{i})=b_{j}
implies
i\leq j
;

\blacktriangleright

there are at most
r
vertices
a_{i}\in A^{\prime}
with
\pi(a_{i})\neq b_{i}
; and

\blacktriangleright

for all
a_{i},a_{j}\in A^{\prime},
if
a_{i}\leq_{A}a_{j},
then
\pi(a_{i})\leq_{B}\pi(a_{j}).

Let
H
be a digraph,
A=\mathchoice{\left(a_{1},\dots,a_{n}\right)}{(a_{1},\dots,a_{n})}{(a_{1},\dots,a_{n})}{(a_{1},\dots,a_{n})},B=\mathchoice{\left(b_{1},\dots,b_{m}\right)}{(b_{1},\dots,b_{m})}{(b_{1},\dots,b_{m})}{(b_{1},\dots,b_{m})}\subseteq V\mathchoice{\left(H\right)}{(H)}{(H)}{(H)}
be ordered sets and let
r\in\mathds{N}.

We say that
A
is
r
-
order-linked
to
B
in
H
if for every
A^{\prime}\subseteq A
and every
B^{\prime}\subseteq B
with
|A^{\prime}|=|B^{\prime}|
where
B^{\prime}
is an
r
-shift of
A^{\prime}
witnessed by the bijection
\pi
there is an
A^{\prime}
-
B^{\prime}
-linkage
\mathcal{L}
in
H
satisfying
\pi\mathchoice{\left(a\right)}{(a)}{(a)}{(a)}=\mathcal{L}\mathchoice{\left(a\right)}{(a)}{(a)}{(a)}
for all
a\in A^{\prime}.

We now define two central objects in our proofs: the paths of well-linked sets and the paths of order-linked sets.

Definition 3.1

(path of
r
-order-linked
/
well-linked
sets
[
16
]
)
.

A
path of
r
-order-linked
/
well-linked
sets
of width
w
and length
\ell
is a tuple
\mathchoice{\left(\mathcal{S},\mathscr{P}\right)}{(\mathcal{S},\mathscr{P})}{(\mathcal{S},\mathscr{P})}{(\mathcal{S},\mathscr{P})}
such that

\blacktriangleright

\mathcal{S}
is a sequence of
\ell+1
pairwise disjoint subgraphs
\mathchoice{\left(S_{0},\dots,S_{\ell}\right)}{(S_{0},\dots,S_{\ell})}{(S_{0},\dots,S_{\ell})}{(S_{0},\dots,S_{\ell})},
which are called
clusters
,

\blacktriangleright

for every
0\leq i\leq\ell
there are disjoint
ordered
sets
A(S_{i}),B(S_{i})\subseteq V\mathchoice{\left(S_{i}\right)}{(S_{i})}{(S_{i})}{(S_{i})}
of size
w
such that
A(S_{i})
is
r
-order-linked
/
well-linked
to
B(S_{i})
in
S_{i},

\blacktriangleright

\mathscr{P}
is a sequence of
\ell
pairwise disjoint linkages
\mathchoice{\left(\mathcal{P}_{0},\mathcal{P}_{1},\dots,\mathcal{P}_{\ell-1}\right)}{(\mathcal{P}_{0},\mathcal{P}_{1},\dots,\mathcal{P}_{\ell-1})}{(\mathcal{P}_{0},\mathcal{P}_{1},\dots,\mathcal{P}_{\ell-1})}{(\mathcal{P}_{0},\mathcal{P}_{1},\dots,\mathcal{P}_{\ell-1})}
such that, for every
0\leq i<\ell,

\mathcal{P}_{i}
is a
B(S_{i})
-
A(S_{i+1})
-linkage of order
w
which is internally disjoint from
S_{i}
and
S_{i+1}
and disjoint from every
S\in\mathcal{S}\setminus\{S_{i},S_{i+1}\}.

Further, a
path of
r
-order-linked sets

\mathchoice{\left(\mathcal{S},\mathscr{P}\right)}{(\mathcal{S},\mathscr{P})}{(\mathcal{S},\mathscr{P})}{(\mathcal{S},\mathscr{P})}
is called
uniform
if for all
0\leq i<\ell
and for all
b_{1},b_{2}\in B(S_{i})
we have that
b_{1}\leq_{B(S_{i})}b_{2}
implies
\mathcal{P}_{i}(b_{1})\leq_{A(S_{i+1})}\mathcal{P}_{i}(b_{2}).

A
path of well-linked sets
is called
strict
if every vertex in
S_{i}
lies on an
A(S_{i})
-
B(S_{i})
-path.

Just as we can find a large fence inside a large acyclic grid, we can also construct a path of well-linked sets from a path of order-linked sets.

Lemma 3.2

(
[
16
, Lemma 8.3]
)
.

Let
{\color[rgb]{0.05,0.375,0.0375}\mathsf{w}_{\ref{proposition:order-linked to path of well-linked sets}}}\mathchoice{\left(w,\ell\right)}{(w,\ell)}{(w,\ell)}{(w,\ell)}\coloneqq w(\ell+1)
.

†
†
margin:

{\color[rgb]{0.05,0.375,0.0375}\mathsf{w}_{\ref{prop-abbr:order-linked to path of well-linked sets}}}

Every path of
w
-order-linked sets
(\mathcal{S}=\mathchoice{\left(S_{0},S_{1},\dots,S_{\ell}\right)}{(S_{0},S_{1},\dots,S_{\ell})}{(S_{0},S_{1},\dots,S_{\ell})}{(S_{0},S_{1},\dots,S_{\ell})}
,
\mathscr{P}=\mathchoice{\left(\mathcal{P}_{0},\mathcal{P}_{1},\dots,\mathcal{P}_{\ell-1}\right)}{(\mathcal{P}_{0},\mathcal{P}_{1},\dots,\mathcal{P}_{\ell-1})}{(\mathcal{P}_{0},\mathcal{P}_{1},\dots,\mathcal{P}_{\ell-1})}{(\mathcal{P}_{0},\mathcal{P}_{1},\dots,\mathcal{P}_{\ell-1})})
of width at least
{\color[rgb]{0.05,0.375,0.0375}\mathsf{w}_{\ref{proposition:order-linked to path of well-linked sets}}}\mathchoice{\left(w,\ell\right)}{(w,\ell)}{(w,\ell)}{(w,\ell)}
and length at least
\ell
contains a path of well-linked sets

one can increase the order of linkedness of a path of order-linked sets by merging several clusters.

One of the main results of

See
1.1

We often need to find linkages inside paths of well-linked or order-linked sets.
We collect in the two lemmas and the observation below all the different cases we need,
proving that the required linkages exist.

3.1
Temporal digraphs and routings

[
16
]
introduced a framework based on temporal digraphs
in order to construct paths of order-linked and of well-linked sets.
This framework is particularly useful when we are working with some
linkage
\mathcal{L}
intersecting a sequence
H_{1},H_{2},\ldots,H_{t}
of disjoint subgraphs in order.
Since there might be no path from
H_{i}
to
H_{j}
if
i>j
,
each
H_{i}
behaves somewhat like a layer of a temporal digraph,
allowing us to reroute some path going through
\mathcal{L}
.

In order to better understand the connectivity given by the
H_{i}
described above,
we need very precise ways of analysing the connectivity available in a temporal digraph.
Towards this end,
[
16
]
defines the concept of
H
-routings
, which we recall here as well.

A
temporal digraph
is a pair
T=(V,\mathcal{A})
consisting of a vertex set
V
and sequence of arc sets
\mathcal{A}=\mathchoice{\left(A_{1},A_{2},\dots A_{\ell}\right)}{(A_{1},A_{2},\dots A_{\ell})}{(A_{1},A_{2},\dots A_{\ell})}{(A_{1},A_{2},\dots A_{\ell})}
such that
D_{t}\mathchoice{\left(T\right)}{(T)}{(T)}{(T)}\coloneqq\mathchoice{\left(V,A_{t}\right)}{(V,A_{t})}{(V,A_{t})}{(V,A_{t})}
is a digraph for all
1\leq t\leq\ell
.
We also refer to
D_{t}\mathchoice{\left(T\right)}{(T)}{(T)}{(T)}
as
layer
t
of
T
and call
t
a
time step
.
The
lifetime
of
D
is given by
\ell\mathchoice{\left(D\right)}{(D)}{(D)}{(D)}\coloneqq\ell
.

Definition 3.7
.

Let
H
be a digraph,
D
be a digraph or temporal digraph, and let
S\subseteq V\mathchoice{\left(D\right)}{(D)}{(D)}{(D)}
.
An
H
-routing (over
S
)
is a bijection
\varphi:V\mathchoice{\left(H\right)}{(H)}{(H)}{(H)}\to S
such that for each
u
-
v
-path
P
in
H
we can find a
\varphi(u)
-
\varphi(v)
-path (or temporal path, resp.) in
D
which is disjoint from
S\setminus\varphi\mathchoice{\left(V\mathchoice{\left(P\right)}{(P)}{(P)}{(P)}\right)}{(V\mathchoice{\left(P\right)}{(P)}{(P)}{(P)})}{(V\mathchoice{\left(P\right)}{(P)}{(P)}{(P)})}{(V\mathchoice{\left(P\right)}{(P)}{(P)}{(P)})}
.

We apply the framework on temporal digraphs in our setting by constructing a
routing temporal digraph
,
defined below and illustrated in
fig.

5
.

Definition 3.8
.

Let
\mathcal{P}
be a linkage and let
\mathcal{Q}=\{Q_{1},Q_{2},\dots,Q_{q}\}
be a set of pairwise disjoint digraphs such that each path
P_{i}\in\mathcal{P}
can be partitioned as
P_{i}^{1}\cdot P_{i}^{2}\cdot\ldots\cdot P_{i}^{q}=P_{i}
such that
V\mathchoice{\left(P_{i}^{j}\right)}{(P_{i}^{j})}{(P_{i}^{j})}{(P_{i}^{j})}\cap V\mathchoice{\left(\mathcal{Q}\right)}{(\mathcal{Q})}{(\mathcal{Q})}{(\mathcal{Q})}\subseteq V\mathchoice{\left(Q_{j}\right)}{(Q_{j})}{(Q_{j})}{(Q_{j})}
for all
1\leq j\leq q
.

The
routing temporal digraph
\mathchoice{\left(V,\mathcal{A}\right)}{(V,\mathcal{A})}{(V,\mathcal{A})}{(V,\mathcal{A})}
of
\mathcal{P}
through
\mathcal{Q}
is constructed as follows.
We set
V=\mathcal{P}
and for each
1\leq j\leq q
we define
A_{j}=\{(P_{a},P_{b})\mid P_{a},P_{b}\in\mathcal{P}
and there is a path from
V\mathchoice{\left(P_{a}\right)}{(P_{a})}{(P_{a})}{(P_{a})}
to
V\mathchoice{\left(P_{b}\right)}{(P_{b})}{(P_{b})}{(P_{b})}
inside
Q_{j}
which is internally disjoint from
\mathcal{P}
\}
.

Figure 5
:
The layers
D_{j}(T)
of the temporal graph
T\coloneqq\mathchoice{\left(V=\{a,b,c\},\mathcal{A}=\{A_{1},A_{2},A_{3}\}\right)}{(V=\{a,b,c\},\mathcal{A}=\{A_{1},A_{2},A_{3}\})}{(V=\{a,b,c\},\mathcal{A}=\{A_{1},A_{2},A_{3}\})}{(V=\{a,b,c\},\mathcal{A}=\{A_{1},A_{2},A_{3}\})}
constructed from the graphs
Q_{j}
displayed above as defined in
definition

3.8
.

A routing of a path in a routing temporal digraph can be used to obtain order-linkedness between two sets.

3.2
Cycles of well-linked sets and cylindrical grids

In this section, we introduce an abstraction of cylindrical grids.

Definition 3.10

(cycle of well-linked sets)
.

A
cycle of well-linked sets
of width
w
and length
\ell
is a tuple
\mathchoice{\left(\mathcal{S},\mathscr{P}\right)}{(\mathcal{S},\mathscr{P})}{(\mathcal{S},\mathscr{P})}{(\mathcal{S},\mathscr{P})}
such that

\blacktriangleright

\mathcal{S}
is a sequence of
\ell
pairwise disjoint subgraphs
\mathchoice{\left(S_{0},\dots,S_{\ell-1}\right)}{(S_{0},\dots,S_{\ell-1})}{(S_{0},\dots,S_{\ell-1})}{(S_{0},\dots,S_{\ell-1})}
, which are called
clusters
,

\blacktriangleright

for every
0\leq i<\ell
there are disjoint sets
A(S_{i}),B(S_{i})\subseteq V\mathchoice{\left(S_{i}\right)}{(S_{i})}{(S_{i})}{(S_{i})}
of size
w
such that
A(S_{i})
is well-linked to
B(S_{i})
in
S_{i}
,

\blacktriangleright

\mathscr{P}
is a sequence of
\ell
pairwise disjoint linkages
\mathchoice{\left(\mathcal{P}_{0},\mathcal{P}_{1},\dots,\mathcal{P}_{\ell-1}\right)}{(\mathcal{P}_{0},\mathcal{P}_{1},\dots,\mathcal{P}_{\ell-1})}{(\mathcal{P}_{0},\mathcal{P}_{1},\dots,\mathcal{P}_{\ell-1})}{(\mathcal{P}_{0},\mathcal{P}_{1},\dots,\mathcal{P}_{\ell-1})}
such that, for every
0\leq i<\ell
,
\mathcal{P}_{i}
is a
B(S_{i})
-
A(S_{(i+1\operatorname{mod}\ell)})
-linkage of order
w
which is internally disjoint from
S_{i}
and
S_{i+1}
and is disjoint from every
S\in\mathcal{S}\setminus\{S_{i},S_{i+1}\}
.

As before, we call
(\mathcal{S},\mathscr{P})

strict
if in every cluster
S_{i}
every vertex
v\in V(S_{i})
is contained in an
A(S_{i})
-
B(S_{i})
-path.

Note that a
cycle of well-linked sets
of width
w
and length
\ell
is a pair
(\mathcal{S},\mathscr{P}\cup\{\mathcal{P}_{\ell}\})
where
(\mathcal{S},\mathscr{P})
is a path of well-linked sets of width
w
and length
\ell-1
, and
\mathcal{P}_{\ell}
is a linkage from the
B
-set of the last cluster to the
A
-set of the first cluster that is internally disjoint from
(\mathcal{S},\mathscr{P})
,
similarly to how a cylindrical grid is a fence plus a linkage from the right side of the fence to its left side which is internally disjoint from the fence.

A useful property of a
(p,q)
-fence
(\mathcal{P},\mathcal{Q})
is that if
A\subseteq\mathsf{start}\mathchoice{\left(\mathcal{P}\right)}{(\mathcal{P})}{(\mathcal{P})}{(\mathcal{P})}
and
B\subseteq\mathsf{end}\mathchoice{\left(\mathcal{P}\right)}{(\mathcal{P})}{(\mathcal{P})}{(\mathcal{P})}
are sets with
|A|=|B|\leq p
then there
is an
A{-}B
-linkage
\mathcal{L}
of order
|A|
in the graph
\bigcup\mathcal{P}\cup\bigcup\mathcal{Q}
.
In terms of connectivity, a path of well-linked sets displays essentially the same properties as a fence.
The following theorem formalises this intuition, showing that we can always find a large fence inside a large
path of well-linked sets.

Theorem 3.10

(
[
16
, Theorem 8.6]
)
.

There exist two functions
†
†
margin:

{\color[rgb]{0.05,0.375,0.0375}\mathsf{w}_{\ref{thm-abbr:poss-to-fence}}}

{\color[rgb]{0.05,0.375,0.0375}\mathsf{w}_{\ref{thm:poss-to-fence}}}\mathchoice{\left(p,q\right)}{(p,q)}{(p,q)}{(p,q)}\in\mathcal{O}(p^{5}q^{5})
, and
†
†
margin:

{\color[rgb]{0.05,0.375,0.0375}\mathsf{\ell}_{\ref{thm-abbr:poss-to-fence}}}

{\color[rgb]{0.05,0.375,0.0375}\mathsf{\ell}_{\ref{thm:poss-to-fence}}}\mathchoice{\left(p,q\right)}{(p,q)}{(p,q)}{(p,q)}\in\operatorname{exp}_{2}^{1}(\operatorname{poly}^{5}(p,q))
such that the following holds.
Every path of a well-linked sets
\mathchoice{\left(\mathcal{S}=(S_{0},S_{1},\ldots,\allowbreak S_{\ell}),\mathscr{P}\right)}{(\mathcal{S}=(S_{0},S_{1},\ldots,\allowbreak S_{\ell}),\mathscr{P})}{(\mathcal{S}=(S_{0},S_{1},\ldots,\allowbreak S_{\ell}),\mathscr{P})}{(\mathcal{S}=(S_{0},S_{1},\ldots,\allowbreak S_{\ell}),\mathscr{P})}
of width at least
{\color[rgb]{0.05,0.375,0.0375}\mathsf{w}_{\ref{thm-abbr:poss-to-fence}}}\mathchoice{\left(p,q\right)}{(p,q)}{(p,q)}{(p,q)}
and length
\ell\geq{\color[rgb]{0.05,0.375,0.0375}\mathsf{\ell}_{\ref{thm-abbr:poss-to-fence}}}\mathchoice{\left(p,q\right)}{(p,q)}{(p,q)}{(p,q)}
contains a
(p,q)
-fence
(\mathcal{P},\mathcal{Q})
.
Moreover,
\mathsf{start}\mathchoice{\left(\mathcal{Q}\right)}{(\mathcal{Q})}{(\mathcal{Q})}{(\mathcal{Q})}\subseteq A(S_{0})
and
\mathsf{end}\mathchoice{\left(\mathcal{Q}\right)}{(\mathcal{Q})}{(\mathcal{Q})}{(\mathcal{Q})}\subseteq B(S_{\ell})
.

In the same way as a path of well-linked sets can be constructed from a fence
[
16
]
, a cycle of well-linked sets can easily be constructed from a cylindrical grid.
We now turn to the converse operation, that is, how one can construct a cylindrical grid from a cycle of well-linked sets.
We first need the following lemma from
[
21
]
.

Lemma 3.11

(
[
21
, Lemma 6.3]
)
.

Let
t
be an integer, let
(\mathcal{P},\mathcal{Q})
be a
(q,q)
-fence where
q\geq{\color[rgb]{0.05,0.375,0.0375}\mathsf{q}_{\ref{lemma:fence-plus-disjoint-back-linkage-implies-cylindrical-grid}}}\mathchoice{\left(t\right)}{(t)}{(t)}{(t)}\coloneqq(t-1)(2t-1)+1
and let
\mathcal{R}
be an
\mathsf{end}\mathchoice{\left(\mathcal{P}\right)}{(\mathcal{P})}{(\mathcal{P})}{(\mathcal{P})}
-
\mathsf{start}\mathchoice{\left(\mathcal{P}\right)}{(\mathcal{P})}{(\mathcal{P})}{(\mathcal{P})}
-linkage of order
q
which is internally disjoint from
(\mathcal{P},\mathcal{Q})
. Then
(\mathcal{P},\mathcal{Q})
contains a cylindrical grid of order
t
as a butterfly minor.

Combining
lemmas

3.11
and
3.6
, we obtain the following.

Theorem 3.11
.

There exist functions

†
†
margin:

{\color[rgb]{0.05,0.375,0.0375}\mathsf{w}_{\ref{thm:coss contains grid}}}

{\color[rgb]{0.05,0.375,0.0375}\mathsf{w}_{\ref{thm:coss contains grid}}}\mathchoice{\left(k\right)}{(k)}{(k)}{(k)}\in\mathcal{O}({k^{20}})
and

†
†
margin:

{\color[rgb]{0.05,0.375,0.0375}\mathsf{\ell}_{\ref{thm:coss contains grid}}}

Proof 3.12
.

Note that

and length at least

.

,

and

.
Let

be the set of paths satisfying
\mathsf{end}\mathchoice{\left(\mathcal{R}^{1}\right)}{(\mathcal{R}^{1})}{(\mathcal{R}^{1})}{(\mathcal{R}^{1})}=\mathsf{start}\mathchoice{\left(\mathcal{Q}^{1}\right)}{(\mathcal{Q}^{1})}{(\mathcal{Q}^{1})}{(\mathcal{Q}^{1})}

be an

-

-linkage of order

.
By

3.5
item

(L1)
, such a linkage

exists.

is internally disjoint from

.
By

,

and

together contain a cylindrical grid of order

as a butterfly minor.

With the results of this section, we now have suitable abstractions of fences and cylindrical grids at hand.
We have also seen how to obtain a cylindrical grid from a cycle of well-linked sets.
What remains to show is how to find a cycle of well-linked sets in a given digraph of large directed treewidth.
We address this problem in the remainder of the paper.

4
The 2-horizontal web

We construct a cycle of well-linked sets from
a path of well-linked sets where the last cluster
is well-linked to the first cluster.
Despite the similarities of the statements, we cannot directly use many of the results of
[
21
]

since several of their steps depend on the functions of
[
21
, Corollary 5.19]
,
which is a power tower whose height depends on the order of the cylindrical grid desired,
and is hence too large for the bounds we wish to obtain here.

Nevertheless, the overall structure of our proof is still based on
[
21
]
.
In
section

4.1
, we obtain a path of well-linked sets with a back-linkage intersecting it
cluster-by-cluster
(or we obtain a cycle of well-linked sets).
Then, in
section

4.2

we obtain
horizontal webs
, which mimic the cluster-by-cluster property above.

4.1
Back-linkage intersecting cluster-by-cluster

We start with the case where the back-linkage is mostly disjoint from the
path of well-linked sets.
With the following simple observation, we can
construct a shorter path of well-linked sets and still have a
back-linkage for it.
This will be then used in
lemma

4.2

in order to construct a cycle of well-linked sets.

Observation 4.1
.

Let
{\color[rgb]{0.05,0.375,0.0375}\mathsf{w}_{\ref{obs:restricting_length_poss}}}\mathchoice{\left(w\right)}{(w)}{(w)}{(w)}\coloneqq 2w
.
†
†
margin:

{\color[rgb]{0.05,0.375,0.0375}\mathsf{w}_{\ref{obs:restricting_length_poss}}}

Let
(\mathcal{S}=\mathchoice{\left(S_{0},S_{1},\dots,S_{\ell}\right)}{(S_{0},S_{1},\dots,S_{\ell})}{(S_{0},S_{1},\dots,S_{\ell})}{(S_{0},S_{1},\dots,S_{\ell})},\mathscr{P})
be a path of well-linked sets of width at least
{\color[rgb]{0.05,0.375,0.0375}\mathsf{w}_{\ref{obs:restricting_length_poss}}}\mathchoice{\left(w\right)}{(w)}{(w)}{(w)}
and length
\ell
in a digraph
D
.
Let
\mathcal{R}
be a
B(S_{\ell})
-
A(S_{0})
-linkage of order
{\color[rgb]{0.05,0.375,0.0375}\mathsf{w}_{\ref{obs:restricting_length_poss}}}\mathchoice{\left(w\right)}{(w)}{(w)}{(w)}
.
Let
0\leq i\leq j\leq\ell
.
Then there is a
B(S_{j})
-
A(S_{i})
-linkage
\mathcal{R}^{\prime}
of order
w
such that
\mathchoice{D\!\left(\mathcal{R}^{\prime}\right)}{D\!\left(\mathcal{R}^{\prime}\right)}{D\left(\mathcal{R}^{\prime}\right)}{D\left(\mathcal{R}^{\prime}\right)}\cap\mathchoice{(\mathcal{S},\mathscr{P})\!\left[i,j\right]}{(\mathcal{S},\mathscr{P})\!\left[i,j\right]}{(\mathcal{S},\mathscr{P})\left[i,j\right]}{(\mathcal{S},\mathscr{P})\left[i,j\right]}\subseteq\mathchoice{D\!\left(\mathcal{R}\cup\mathsf{start}\mathchoice{\left(\mathcal{R}^{\prime}\right)}{(\mathcal{R}^{\prime})}{(\mathcal{R}^{\prime})}{(\mathcal{R}^{\prime})}\cup\mathsf{end}\mathchoice{\left(\mathcal{R}^{\prime}\right)}{(\mathcal{R}^{\prime})}{(\mathcal{R}^{\prime})}{(\mathcal{R}^{\prime})}\right)}{D\!\left(\mathcal{R}\cup\mathsf{start}\mathchoice{\left(\mathcal{R}^{\prime}\right)}{(\mathcal{R}^{\prime})}{(\mathcal{R}^{\prime})}{(\mathcal{R}^{\prime})}\cup\mathsf{end}\mathchoice{\left(\mathcal{R}^{\prime}\right)}{(\mathcal{R}^{\prime})}{(\mathcal{R}^{\prime})}{(\mathcal{R}^{\prime})}\right)}{D\left(\mathcal{R}\cup\mathsf{start}\mathchoice{\left(\mathcal{R}^{\prime}\right)}{(\mathcal{R}^{\prime})}{(\mathcal{R}^{\prime})}{(\mathcal{R}^{\prime})}\cup\mathsf{end}\mathchoice{\left(\mathcal{R}^{\prime}\right)}{(\mathcal{R}^{\prime})}{(\mathcal{R}^{\prime})}{(\mathcal{R}^{\prime})}\right)}{D\left(\mathcal{R}\cup\mathsf{start}\mathchoice{\left(\mathcal{R}^{\prime}\right)}{(\mathcal{R}^{\prime})}{(\mathcal{R}^{\prime})}{(\mathcal{R}^{\prime})}\cup\mathsf{end}\mathchoice{\left(\mathcal{R}^{\prime}\right)}{(\mathcal{R}^{\prime})}{(\mathcal{R}^{\prime})}{(\mathcal{R}^{\prime})}\right)}
.

Proof 4.1
.

If
j<\ell
, then by
lemma

3.6
there is a linkage
\mathcal{L}_{B}
from
B(S_{j})
to
B(S_{\ell})
in
\mathchoice{\mathchoice{\left(\mathcal{S},\mathscr{P}\right)}{(\mathcal{S},\mathscr{P})}{(\mathcal{S},\mathscr{P})}{(\mathcal{S},\mathscr{P})}\!\left[j,\ell\right]}{\mathchoice{\left(\mathcal{S},\mathscr{P}\right)}{(\mathcal{S},\mathscr{P})}{(\mathcal{S},\mathscr{P})}{(\mathcal{S},\mathscr{P})}\!\left[j,\ell\right]}{\mathchoice{\left(\mathcal{S},\mathscr{P}\right)}{(\mathcal{S},\mathscr{P})}{(\mathcal{S},\mathscr{P})}{(\mathcal{S},\mathscr{P})}\left[j,\ell\right]}{\mathchoice{\left(\mathcal{S},\mathscr{P}\right)}{(\mathcal{S},\mathscr{P})}{(\mathcal{S},\mathscr{P})}{(\mathcal{S},\mathscr{P})}\left[j,\ell\right]}
which is internally disjoint from
S_{j}
.
If
j=\ell
, we set
\mathcal{L}_{B}
as the linkage containing only the vertices of
B(S_{\ell})
and no arcs.

Similarly, if
i=0
, we set
\mathcal{L}_{A}
as the linkage containing only the vertices of
A(S_{0})
and no arcs.
Otherwise, we set
\mathcal{L}_{A}
as an
A(S_{0})
-
A(S_{i})
-linkage of order
2w
in
\mathchoice{\mathchoice{\left(\mathcal{S},\mathscr{P}\right)}{(\mathcal{S},\mathscr{P})}{(\mathcal{S},\mathscr{P})}{(\mathcal{S},\mathscr{P})}\!\left[0,i\right]}{\mathchoice{\left(\mathcal{S},\mathscr{P}\right)}{(\mathcal{S},\mathscr{P})}{(\mathcal{S},\mathscr{P})}{(\mathcal{S},\mathscr{P})}\!\left[0,i\right]}{\mathchoice{\left(\mathcal{S},\mathscr{P}\right)}{(\mathcal{S},\mathscr{P})}{(\mathcal{S},\mathscr{P})}{(\mathcal{S},\mathscr{P})}\left[0,i\right]}{\mathchoice{\left(\mathcal{S},\mathscr{P}\right)}{(\mathcal{S},\mathscr{P})}{(\mathcal{S},\mathscr{P})}{(\mathcal{S},\mathscr{P})}\left[0,i\right]}
, which exists by

3.6
.

Let
\mathcal{L}=\mathcal{L}_{B}\cdot\mathcal{R}\cdot\mathcal{L}_{A}
.
As
\mathcal{L}_{B}
and
\mathcal{L}_{A}
are internally disjoint, we have that
\mathcal{L}
is a half-integral linkage from
B(S_{j})
to
A(S_{i})
.
By
lemma

2.3
,
\mathchoice{D\!\left(\mathcal{L}\right)}{D\!\left(\mathcal{L}\right)}{D\left(\mathcal{L}\right)}{D\left(\mathcal{L}\right)}
contains a
B(S_{k})
-
A(S_{i})
-linkage
\mathcal{R}^{\prime}
of order
w
.

As both
\mathcal{L}_{A}
and
\mathcal{L}_{B}
are internally disjoint from
\mathchoice{(\mathcal{S},\mathscr{P})\!\left[i,j\right]}{(\mathcal{S},\mathscr{P})\!\left[i,j\right]}{(\mathcal{S},\mathscr{P})\left[i,j\right]}{(\mathcal{S},\mathscr{P})\left[i,j\right]}
, we have that
\mathchoice{D\!\left(\mathcal{R}^{\prime}\right)}{D\!\left(\mathcal{R}^{\prime}\right)}{D\left(\mathcal{R}^{\prime}\right)}{D\left(\mathcal{R}^{\prime}\right)}\cap\mathchoice{(\mathcal{S},\mathscr{P})\!\left[i,j\right]}{(\mathcal{S},\mathscr{P})\!\left[i,j\right]}{(\mathcal{S},\mathscr{P})\left[i,j\right]}{(\mathcal{S},\mathscr{P})\left[i,j\right]}\subseteq\mathchoice{D\!\left(\mathcal{R}\cup\mathsf{start}\mathchoice{\left(\mathcal{R}^{\prime}\right)}{(\mathcal{R}^{\prime})}{(\mathcal{R}^{\prime})}{(\mathcal{R}^{\prime})}\cup\mathsf{end}\mathchoice{\left(\mathcal{R}^{\prime}\right)}{(\mathcal{R}^{\prime})}{(\mathcal{R}^{\prime})}{(\mathcal{R}^{\prime})}\right)}{D\!\left(\mathcal{R}\cup\mathsf{start}\mathchoice{\left(\mathcal{R}^{\prime}\right)}{(\mathcal{R}^{\prime})}{(\mathcal{R}^{\prime})}{(\mathcal{R}^{\prime})}\cup\mathsf{end}\mathchoice{\left(\mathcal{R}^{\prime}\right)}{(\mathcal{R}^{\prime})}{(\mathcal{R}^{\prime})}{(\mathcal{R}^{\prime})}\right)}{D\left(\mathcal{R}\cup\mathsf{start}\mathchoice{\left(\mathcal{R}^{\prime}\right)}{(\mathcal{R}^{\prime})}{(\mathcal{R}^{\prime})}{(\mathcal{R}^{\prime})}\cup\mathsf{end}\mathchoice{\left(\mathcal{R}^{\prime}\right)}{(\mathcal{R}^{\prime})}{(\mathcal{R}^{\prime})}{(\mathcal{R}^{\prime})}\right)}{D\left(\mathcal{R}\cup\mathsf{start}\mathchoice{\left(\mathcal{R}^{\prime}\right)}{(\mathcal{R}^{\prime})}{(\mathcal{R}^{\prime})}{(\mathcal{R}^{\prime})}\cup\mathsf{end}\mathchoice{\left(\mathcal{R}^{\prime}\right)}{(\mathcal{R}^{\prime})}{(\mathcal{R}^{\prime})}{(\mathcal{R}^{\prime})}\right)}
.

If a back-linkage
\mathcal{R}
is completely disjoint
from a contiguous
part
of a path of well-linked sets,
then we can use
4.1
and
3.3

in order to obtain a cycle of well-linked sets, whereas the original back-linkage
becomes essentially one of the linkages between the clusters.

We define

\displaystyle\@add@raggedright\marginpar{\sloppy\raggedright{\color[rgb]{0.05,0.375,0.0375}\mathsf{\ell^{\prime}}_{\ref{lemma:back-linkage avoids many clusters}}}}{\color[rgb]{0.05,0.375,0.0375}\mathsf{\ell^{\prime}}_{\ref{lemma:back-linkage avoids many clusters}}}\mathchoice{\left(\ell\right)}{(\ell)}{(\ell)}{(\ell)}

\displaystyle\coloneqq\ell-1,

\displaystyle\@add@raggedright\marginpar{\sloppy\raggedright{\color[rgb]{0.05,0.375,0.0375}\mathsf{r}_{\ref{lemma:back-linkage avoids many clusters}}}}{\color[rgb]{0.05,0.375,0.0375}\mathsf{w^{\prime}}_{\ref{lemma:back-linkage avoids many clusters}}}\mathchoice{\left(w\right)}{(w)}{(w)}{(w)}

\displaystyle\coloneqq 2w,

Lemma 4.2
.

Let
w,\ell
be integers, let
\mathchoice{\left(\mathcal{S},\mathscr{P}\right)}{(\mathcal{S},\mathscr{P})}{(\mathcal{S},\mathscr{P})}{(\mathcal{S},\mathscr{P})}
be a path of well-linked sets of length
\ell^{\prime}\geq{\color[rgb]{0.05,0.375,0.0375}\mathsf{\ell^{\prime}}_{\ref{lemma:back-linkage avoids many clusters}}}\mathchoice{\left(\ell\right)}{(\ell)}{(\ell)}{(\ell)}
and width
w^{\prime}\geq{\color[rgb]{0.05,0.375,0.0375}\mathsf{w^{\prime}}_{\ref{lemma:back-linkage avoids many clusters}}}\mathchoice{\left(w\right)}{(w)}{(w)}{(w)}
with a partial back-linkage
\mathcal{R}
of order

in a digraph

.
If there is a
0\leq i\leq\ell^{\prime}-\ell+1
such that
\mathcal{R}
is internally disjoint from
\mathchoice{\mathchoice{\left(\mathcal{S},\mathscr{P}\right)}{(\mathcal{S},\mathscr{P})}{(\mathcal{S},\mathscr{P})}{(\mathcal{S},\mathscr{P})}\!\left[i,i+\ell-1\right]}{\mathchoice{\left(\mathcal{S},\mathscr{P}\right)}{(\mathcal{S},\mathscr{P})}{(\mathcal{S},\mathscr{P})}{(\mathcal{S},\mathscr{P})}\!\left[i,i+\ell-1\right]}{\mathchoice{\left(\mathcal{S},\mathscr{P}\right)}{(\mathcal{S},\mathscr{P})}{(\mathcal{S},\mathscr{P})}{(\mathcal{S},\mathscr{P})}\left[i,i+\ell-1\right]}{\mathchoice{\left(\mathcal{S},\mathscr{P}\right)}{(\mathcal{S},\mathscr{P})}{(\mathcal{S},\mathscr{P})}{(\mathcal{S},\mathscr{P})}\left[i,i+\ell-1\right]}
, then
D
contains a cycle of well-linked sets of length
\ell
and width
w
as a subgraph.

Proof 4.3
.

Let

,
\mathchoice{\left(S_{0},S_{1},\dots,S_{\ell^{\prime}}\right)}{(S_{0},S_{1},\dots,S_{\ell^{\prime}})}{(S_{0},S_{1},\dots,S_{\ell^{\prime}})}{(S_{0},S_{1},\dots,S_{\ell^{\prime}})}\coloneqq\mathcal{S}

.

-

By

contains a path of well-linked sets

of width

Hence, by definition,
